I know it’s late at night, but I haven’t noticed whether it starts before I fall asleep or after I’ve been asleep and wake up.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you eat a much larger amount of food than most people would in a similar time and situation, or is it more like continuous small amounts of picking over a longer stretch of time?\n\nPatient: It can be a lot. Some nights after work I’ll eat several tamales, a big bowl of pozole, tortillas, then whatever rice or beans are left. I’m usually standing in the kitchen telling myself I’m just finishing the leftovers, but it adds up to a whole lot of food.\n\nDoctor: The next morning, do you usually remember eating during the night in detail, or do you sometimes have little to no memory of it happening at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid attention to how much I remember the next morning.\n\nDoctor: On the mornings after these nighttime eating episodes, do you find that you have little or no appetite for breakfast?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed whether I’m less hungry for breakfast the next morning.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

To start, can you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m worried about how I am with food late at night. Once I start picking at it, I tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but I keep reaching for more anyway. It feels like I’m not really the one deciding anymore.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing while you eat, or do you sometimes wake up from sleep and find yourself eating with little memory of how it started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I know I’m up late when it happens, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I was fully awake or had been asleep first.\n\nDoctor: Does this eating usually happen in the evening before you've gone to sleep at all, or does it happen after you've already fallen asleep and then wake up in the night?\n\nPatient: I honestly can’t tell you. I know it’s late at night, but I haven’t noticed whether it starts before I fall asleep or after I’ve been asleep and wake up.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you eat a much larger amount of food than most people would in a similar time and situation, or is it more like continuous small amounts of picking over a longer stretch of time?\n\nPatient: It can be a lot. Some nights after work I’ll eat several tamales, a big bowl of pozole, tortillas, then whatever rice or beans are left. I’m usually standing in the kitchen telling myself I’m just finishing the leftovers, but it adds up to a whole lot of food.\n\nDoctor: The next morning, do you usually remember eating during the night in detail, or do you sometimes have little to no memory of it happening at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid attention to how much I remember the next morning.\n\nDoctor: On the mornings after these nighttime eating episodes, do you find that you have little or no appetite for breakfast?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed whether I’m less hungry for breakfast the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else—like a family member or roommate—ever seen you eating during the night and mentioned that you looked like you were asleep, seemed confused, or didn't respond normally when they spoke to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. No one’s ever mentioned seeing me like that, as far as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: While you're actually eating during these episodes, do you feel fully alert and clear-headed, or does it feel more like you're in a dream-like, foggy, or semi-conscious state?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I feel fully awake or kind of foggy when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, can you actually taste and enjoy the food while you're eating it, or do you have no real memory of what it tasted like afterward?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid attention to whether I can taste it much or remember the food afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you remember consciously deciding to get out of bed and go to the kitchen each time, or do you sometimes just find yourself already there eating without recalling how you got up or walked in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about whether I remember deciding to get up or how I got to the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Right before you start eating at night, do you notice a craving or urge that you're aware of, or does the eating seem to start without any warning or awareness on your part?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed whether there’s an urge beforehand or if it just sort of starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever woken up in the morning and found food wrappers, dirty dishes, or missing food that you have absolutely no memory of preparing or eating the night before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed finding things like that in the morning or checked whether I remembered them.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes tend to happen when you're having trouble falling asleep or you've woken up and can't get back to sleep, or do they happen even on nights when your sleep seems to be going fine?\n\nPatient: I honestly haven’t paid close enough attention to tell. No one’s ever mentioned seeing me like that, as far as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: While you're actually eating during these episodes, do you feel fully alert and clear-headed, or does it feel more like you're in a dream-like, foggy, or semi-conscious state?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I feel fully awake or kind of foggy when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, can you actually taste and enjoy the food while you're eating it, or do you have no real memory of what it tasted like afterward?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid attention to whether I can taste it much or remember the food afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you remember consciously deciding to get out of bed and go to the kitchen each time, or do you sometimes just find yourself already there eating without recalling how you got up or walked in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about whether I remember deciding to get up or how I got to the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Right before you start eating at night, do you notice a craving or urge that you're aware of, or does the eating seem to start without any warning or awareness on your part?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed whether there’s an urge beforehand or if it just sort of starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever woken up in the morning and found food wrappers, dirty dishes, or missing food that you have absolutely no memory of preparing or eating the night before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed finding things like that in the morning or checked whether I remembered them.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes tend to happen when you're having trouble falling asleep or you've woken up and can't get back to sleep, or do they happen even on nights when your sleep seems to be going fine?\n\nPatient: I honestly haven’t paid close enough attention to tell. I’m not sure if it lines up with nights I can’t sleep or if it happens when my sleep’s otherwise okay.\n\nDoctor: The next day, after one of these nighttime eating episodes, do you feel upset, guilty, or embarrassed about it, or does it not really bother you emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid much attention to how I feel about it the next day.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you first wake up unable to fall back asleep, and then feel you need to eat before you can fall asleep again?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say. I haven’t noticed whether I wake up first and then feel like I need to eat to get back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: When you're standing in the kitchen eating at night, are you fully awake and aware that you're choosing to eat, even if you feel like you can't stop, or does it feel like you're not conscious at all during that time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed whether I’m fully awake at the time or not.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the eating, have you ever had episodes of sleepwalking or done other things during the night—like moving around, talking, or other activities—that you don't remember afterward, either now or as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, but I honestly haven’t paid much attention to that kind of thing. I can’t remember any episodes like that, either now or when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: On the nights this happens, does the eating occur before you've even gone to bed and tried to fall asleep, or does it happen only after you've already gone to bed and attempted to sleep for the night?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure which it is. I haven’t paid close enough attention to whether I’d already gone to bed first or not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can’t remember any episodes like that, either now or when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: On the nights this happens, does the eating occur before you've even gone to bed and tried to fall asleep, or does it happen only after you've already gone to bed and attempted to sleep for the night?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure which it is. I haven’t really noticed whether I’m less hungry for breakfast the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else—like a family member or roommate—ever seen you eating during the night and mentioned that you looked like you were asleep, seemed confused, or didn't respond normally when they spoke to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. No one’s ever mentioned seeing me like that, as far as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: While you're actually eating during these episodes, do you feel fully alert and clear-headed, or does it feel more like you're in a dream-like, foggy, or semi-conscious state?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I feel fully awake or kind of foggy when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, can you actually taste and enjoy the food while you're eating it, or do you have no real memory of what it tasted like afterward?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid attention to whether I can taste it much or remember the food afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you remember consciously deciding to get out of bed and go to the kitchen each time, or do you sometimes just find yourself already there eating without recalling how you got up or walked in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about whether I remember deciding to get up or how I got to the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Right before you start eating at night, do you notice a craving or urge that you're aware of, or does the eating seem to start without any warning or awareness on your part?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed whether there’s an urge beforehand or if it just sort of starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever woken up in the morning and found food wrappers, dirty dishes, or missing food that you have absolutely no memory of preparing or eating the night before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed finding things like that in the morning or checked whether I remembered them.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes tend to happen when you're having trouble falling asleep or you've woken up and can't get back to sleep, or do they happen even on nights when your sleep seems to be going fine?\n\nPatient: I honestly haven’t paid close enough attention to tell. I’m not sure if it lines up with nights I can’t sleep or if it happens when my sleep’s otherwise okay.\n\nDoctor: The next day, after one of these nighttime eating episodes, do you feel upset, guilty, or embarrassed about it, or does it not really bother you emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid much attention to how I feel about it the next day.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you first wake up unable to fall back asleep, and then feel you need to eat before you can fall asleep again?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say. I haven’t noticed whether I wake up first and then feel like I need to eat to get back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: When you're standing in the kitchen eating at night, are you fully awake and aware that you're choosing to eat, even if you feel like you can't stop, or does it feel like you're not conscious at all during that time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed whether I’m fully awake at the time or not.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the eating, have you ever had episodes of sleepwalking or done other things during the night—like moving around, talking, or other activities—that you don't remember afterward, either now or as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, but I honestly haven’t paid much attention to that kind of thing. I can’t remember any episodes like that, either now or when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: On the nights this happens, does the eating occur before you've even gone to bed and tried to fall asleep, or does it happen only after you've already gone to bed and attempted to sleep for the night?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure which it is. I haven’t paid close enough attention to whether I’d already gone to bed first or not.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these nighttime episodes, do you ever find yourself eating large amounts of food and feeling unable to stop or control it during the daytime as well, or does this loss of control only happen at night?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly late at night. Once I start picking at food, I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but my hand just keeps going back. It feels like I’m not really making the decision anymore.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, would you say you are fully awake and aware of what you're doing, even if you feel unable to stop, or does it feel like you have little to no awareness of what's happening while it's occurring?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really stopped to notice how awake I am when it’s happening."
